<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that fourfold ergodic averages along the times $`n,2n,3n,4n`$ converge almost everywhere to the product of the integrals for every invertible, bimeasurable, mixing probability-preserving transformation and every fixed choice of bounded measurable inputs. Convergence holds along all positive integer averaging lengths. No quantitative mixing rate is assumed, and the probability space need not be standard.

- **claim-main-fourfold-mixing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $(X,\mathcal F,\mu)$ be a probability space and let $T:X\to X$ be invertible, bimeasurable, measure preserving, and mixing in the sense of <a href="#eq:mixing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixing">[eq:mixing]</a>. For every $f_1,f_2,f_3,f_4\in L^\infty(\mu)$, $$\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N f_1(T^nx)f_2(T^{2n}x)f_3(T^{3n}x)f_4(T^{4n}x) \longrightarrow \prod_{j=1}^4\int_X f_j\,d\mu$$ for $\mu$-almost every $x$, along all positive integers $N$. The original probability space need not be standard.
  - Explanation: For an invertible, bimeasurable, measure-preserving, mixing transformation, the fourfold averages along multiples of the step converge almost everywhere to the product of the integrals, along every positive averaging length. The probability space need not be standard and no mixing rate is assumed.
- **lemma-lower-length-convergence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-lower-length)): Let $(Y,m,R)$ be an invertible probability-preserving transformation on a standard probability space. If $\varnothing\ne J\subsetneq I$ and $g_i\in L^\infty(m)$ for $i\in J$, then $$\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\prod_{i\in J}g_i(R^{in}y)$$ converges for $m$-almost every $y$.
  - Explanation: Averages over any proper subset of the four times converge almost everywhere on an arbitrary invertible system on a standard space, with neither ergodicity nor mixing assumed.
- **prop-relative-structure** (classification, [source](#stmt-prop-structure)): The following statements hold for standard probability-preserving systems.* 1. *There is a maximal measure-distal factor $W_Y$. It contains the invariant factor, and $Y\to W_Y$ is relatively weakly mixing. A distal factor pulls back into the maximal distal factor of any extension. Every measure-preserving automorphism commuting with $R$ preserves $W_Y$.* 2. *Factors and joinings of distal systems are distal. A distal system is relatively distal over each of its factors. For every nonzero integer $q$, $R$ is distal if and only if $R^q$ is distal.* 3. *Relative weak mixing passes to nonzero integer powers and to conditionally independent base change. More generally, the product of relatively weakly mixing fibre laws over any invariant joining of their base laws is relatively weakly mixing over that joining.* 4. *A relatively weakly mixing extension and a relatively distal extension of the same base are relatively disjoint: the only invariant joining which identifies their base coordinates is the relatively independent joining.* 5. *If $R$ is distal, then, for every $d\ge1$ and bounded $g_1,\ldots,g_d$, the averages $$\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\prod_{i=1}^d g_i(R^{in}y)$$ converge almost surely.* *If $(S_Y^t)_{t\in\mathbb R}$ is a probability-preserving flow, the maximal distal factor $W_Y$ of $S_Y^1$ is a flow factor. Its action at every nonzero rational time is distal.
  - Explanation: On standard systems there is a maximal measure-distal factor over which the system is relatively weakly mixing. Distality and relative weak mixing behave well under powers, factors, joinings and base change, relatively weakly mixing and relatively distal extensions are relatively disjoint, and distal systems have pointwise convergent multiple averages.
- **lemma-relative-norm-characteristic** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-relative-norm)): Suppose $Y\to B$ is relatively weakly mixing for an invertible transformation $R$. If $a_1,\ldots,a_d$ are distinct nonzero integers and $g_1,\ldots,g_d$ are bounded, then $$\label{eq:relative-norm} \left\\|\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N \left(\prod_{i=1}^d g_i\circ R^{a_i n} -\prod_{i=1}^d\mathbb E[g_i\mid B]\circ R^{a_i n}\right)\right\\|_2 \longrightarrow0.$$
  - Explanation: Over a relatively weakly mixing extension, multiple averages along distinct nonzero integer multiples are asymptotically equal in $L^2$ to the same averages of the conditional expectations on the base.
- **prop-selectors-witnessing-failure** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-selectors)): If Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> fails, there are real functions $f_i\in L^\infty(\mu)$ with $\\|f_i\\|_\infty\le1$ and $\int f_i\,\,d\mu=0$, a finite set $F\subset[1,2]$, measurable functions $$N_a:C\longrightarrow\{c2^k:c\in F,\ k\ge a\},\qquad a\ge1,$$ a continuous real function $\Psi$ on $(\mathbb R/\mathbb Z)^I$, and $\epsilon_0>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $a$, $$\label{eq:selected-positive} \int_C\frac1{N_a(z)}\int_0^{N_a(z)} \Psi\bigl((\upsilon(S_C^{it}z))_{i\in I}\bigr) \prod_{i\in I}f_i\bigl(\xi(S_C^{it}z)\bigr) \,\,dt\,\,dm_C(z)\ge\epsilon_0.$$ The selectors may be chosen independent of the initial phase.
  - Explanation: If the main theorem fails, one obtains centred bounded real functions, a finite grid of length multipliers, measurable selectors of averaging lengths, a continuous phase test and a positive lower bound on a selected correlation.
- **prop-selected-laws** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-selected)): Formula <a href="#eq:selected-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-law">[eq:selected-law]</a> defines a probability measure $\lambda_Y$ on $Y^I$ with the following properties.* 1. *Every marginal is $m_Y$, and $\lambda_Y$ is invariant under $(y_i)_i\mapsto(S_Y^{it}y_i)_i$ for every $t\in\mathbb R$. If $\theta:Y'\to Y$ is an extension over $C$, then $(\theta^I)_*\lambda_{Y'}=\lambda_Y$. These projections also hold for countable inverse limits of extensions.* 2. *The law $\Lambda_Y$ of $P$ is its deterministic-length law: for bounded $H_i$ on $W_Y$, $$\label{eq:ordinary-parameter-law} \int\prod_iH_i(w_i)\,\,d\Lambda_Y =\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_Y\frac1L\int_0^L \prod_i H_i(\omega_Y(S_Y^{it}y))\,\,dt\,\,dm_Y(y).$$* 3. *For every proper subset $J\subsetneq I$, the conditional law of $(y_i)_{i\in J}$ given the *entire* parameter array $P$ is $$\label{eq:proper-fibres} \operatorname{Law}_{\lambda_Y}\bigl((y_i)_{i\in J}\mid P\bigr) =\bigotimes_{i\in J}m_{Y,P_i}.$$* *In a countable inverse limit, functions pulled back from the selected laws at finite stages are dense in the $L^2$ space of the limiting selected law.
  - Explanation: The ultrafilter limit along selected lengths defines a probability law on four outputs with the original marginals, flow invariance and functoriality under extensions. Its parameter law is the deterministic-length law, and proper sub-tuples are conditionally product measures given the whole distal parameter array.
- **lemma-parameter-distal-normalized** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-parameter-distal)): The full parameter array $(\omega_Y(y_i))_{i\in I}$ is a distal factor of the time-one map of $\mathsf M_b(Y)$, and hence belongs to its maximal distal factor. The same assertion holds for the arrays of original parameters in every finite iteration of normalized selected flows.
  - Explanation: After normalizing the flow speed at one slot, the full array of distal parameters is still a distal factor, also under finite iterations of the construction.
- **thm-tensor-packing** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-packing)): There is a universal constant $C$ with the following property. Let $0<\varepsilon\le1$, $d\ge1$, and $L\ge0$ be given, with $d,L$ integers. Let $x_1,\ldots,x_m$ and $y_1,\ldots,y_m$ lie in the unit balls of real Hilbert spaces $H_1,H_2$. For each family suppose that a graph on $\{1,\ldots,m\}$ of maximum degree at most $L$ contains every off-diagonal pair whose absolute inner product exceeds $\eta$, where $$0\le\eta\le\varepsilon^2/10^4.$$ The two exceptional graphs may differ. For every orthogonal projection $P$ on $H_1\otimes H_2$ of rank at most $d$, $$\label{eq:packing-quantitative} \#\{n:\lVert P(x_n\otimes y_n)\rVert\ge\varepsilon\} \le \frac{C(1+L)}{\varepsilon^2} \exp\!\left(C\varepsilon^{-2}\sqrt d\log(2+d)\right).$$ In particular, for $$\label{eq:packing-dimension} d_m=\left\lceil(1+\log m)^{3/2}\right\rceil,$$ the left side is $o_{\varepsilon,L}(m)$, uniformly over the Hilbert spaces, the vectors, the projections, and the allowed values of $\eta$.
  - Explanation: For almost orthogonal families outside a bounded-degree exceptional graph, only a controlled number of tensor products can have large projection onto a subspace of bounded rank. The bound is independent of the ambient Hilbert-space dimensions.
- **lemma-packing-gaussian-matrix** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-packing-gaussian)): Let $B_1,\ldots,B_r$ be matrices between two finite-dimensional real Hilbert spaces, and put $$H_j=\begin{pmatrix}0&B_j\\ B_j^*&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad S=\sum_{j=1}^r H_j^2.$$ If $\lVert S\rVert_{\rm op}\le\sqrt d$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S\le2d$ for some $d\ge1$, then for independent standard normal variables $g_j$, $$\label{eq:packing-gaussian-bound} \mathbb E\left\\|\sum_{j=1}^r g_jB_j\right\\|_{\rm op} \le C d^{1/4}\sqrt{\log(2+d)}.$$ The constant does not depend on the dimensions of the two spaces.
  - Explanation: If the quadratic sum of the block matrices has controlled operator norm and trace, the expected operator norm of the Gaussian combination is bounded independently of the dimensions of the two spaces.
- **lemma-channel-distal-conditioning** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-channel-distal)): For a channel $Y$, both conditional product identities in <a href="#eq:channel-all" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:channel-all">[eq:channel-all]</a>–<a href="#eq:channel-proper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:channel-proper">[eq:channel-proper]</a> also hold when conditioning on its maximal measure-distal factor $W_Y$. Every flow extension of $Y$, equipped with the pulled-back original factor and outputs, is a channel and satisfies the same assertion for its own maximal distal factor.
  - Explanation: The two conditional product identities defining a channel remain valid when conditioning on the maximal measure-distal factor, and every extension with pulled-back outputs is again a channel.
- **prop-channel-closure** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-channel-closure)): Let $Y$ be a channel and $b\in I$. On $\mathsf M_b(Y)$ use $\pi_Y(y_b)$ as original factor. For each $j\in\{1,2,3\}$, let the new group $j$ contain the outputs $D_\alpha(y_i)$ with $\alpha\in\mathcal D_j$ and $i\in I$. With these factors, $\mathsf M_b(Y)$ is a channel.
  - Explanation: The normalized selected-law flow of a channel is again a channel when the outputs of all slots are copied into the three groups. Proper collections of groups use mixing; the full triple uses tensor packing.
- **cor-channel-array-distal** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-channel-array-distal)): In Proposition <a href="#prop:channel-closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:channel-closure">12</a>, let $P=(\omega_Y(y_i))_{i\in I}$ be the array of old distal parameters. The spatial coordinates of all copied outputs are independent with their product law conditionally on $P$. The corresponding assertion holds after any finite iteration of that proposition, conditionally on the complete array of old $W_Y$-parameters.
  - Explanation: The spatial coordinates of all copied outputs have their product law conditionally on the array of old distal parameters, also after finitely many iterations.
- **prop-role-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-role-bound)): Every nonzero correlation <a href="#eq:role-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:role-correlation">[eq:role-correlation]</a> has at most two output roles. In particular, the maximal number $r$ just defined satisfies $r\le2$.
  - Explanation: A nonzero correlation cannot use more than two output roles. Four roles are excluded by independence, and three by Bessel's inequality along coordinate lines of an iterated array.
- **prop-saturation** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-saturation)): Every standard flow over $C$ has a standard extension $Y$ with the following property. For every further standard extension $\rho:Y'\to Y$ over $C$, every $f\in L^2(\lambda_Y)$, and every $l\in\{0\}\cup I$, $$\label{eq:saturation-identity} \mathbb E_{\lambda_{Y'}}[f\circ\rho^I\mid\mathcal D_l(Y')] =\mathbb E_{\lambda_Y}[f\mid\mathcal D_l(Y)]\circ\rho^I.$$ If the initial flow is a channel, the extension is a channel with the same outputs pulled back to it.
  - Explanation: Every standard flow over the suspension has a saturated extension on which the relevant conditional expectations do not change under further extensions; it is built by an energy-maximization argument and preserves channel structure.
- **lemma-regrouped-channel** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-regrouped-channel)): With these outputs and groups, $Z$ is a channel over its slot-$b$ copy of $C$.
  - Explanation: Regrouping the outputs of the normalized selected flow into three new groups gives a channel; the full product law uses maximality of the number of output roles.
- **lemma-square-conditional** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-square-conditional)): In the square just defined, the conditional law of column $b$ given $E$ is its $\lambda_Y$ law given $p$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:square-row-locality} \mathbb E_{\lambda_Z}[q(\mathbf z_b)\mid E,(y_i)_{i\in I}] =u(E_b,y_b).$$ In particular, the conditional law of $y_b$ given $E$ is $m_Y(\,dy\mid w)$; in a single $Z$-state, the conditional law of its slot-$b$ state given $e=\omega_Z$ is the same fibre law over its old slot-$b$ parameter.
  - Explanation: Using saturation, the conditional law of the distinguished column given the new parameters is the old selected law given the old parameters, and the row factor's conditional expectation depends only on the corner state and its parameter.
- **lemma-parameter-reanchoring** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-parameter-reanchoring)): Under $\lambda_Z$, the variables $p$ and $E_b$ are conditionally independent given $w=P_{bb}$.
  - Explanation: Given the corner parameter, the old column parameter array and the new row parameter are conditionally independent.
- **lemma-independent-hilbert-vectors** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-independent-hilbert-vectors)): Let $V$ be a square-integrable random vector in a separable real Hilbert space, and let $V'$ be an independent copy. If $\langle V,V'\rangle=0$ almost surely, then $V=0$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: A random Hilbert-space vector almost surely orthogonal to an independent copy of itself vanishes almost surely.
- **prop-activation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-activation)): For the saturated channel $Y$ and maximal nonzero correlation chosen above, the regrouped channel $Z$ has a nonzero correlation <a href="#eq:role-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:role-correlation">[eq:role-correlation]</a> with output roles $A\cup\{b\}$.
  - Explanation: For the saturated channel and a maximal correlation, the regrouped channel carries a nonzero correlation with one more output role, contradicting maximality.
- **prop-ll-depth-local-input** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-ll-depth)): Let $S=\{0\}\cup J$, where $J$ is a three-element subset of $\{1,2,3,4\}$. Let $\mathcal T$ be a finite rooted dyadic tree with root $K_*$ and largest depth $d$, and let $\mathcal K\subset\mathcal T$ have kernels satisfying <a href="#eq:ll-kernels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ll-kernels">[eq:ll-kernels]</a>. For each $j\in S$, let $(z_{j,s})_{0\le s\le d}$ be measurable scalar functions on $K_*$. Extend these functions by zero outside $K_*$. Suppose that $\{0,\ldots,d\}$ has a deterministic partition into $W_j\ge1$ nonempty consecutive blocks, on each of which $$\mathcal V_B(z_j)(y)\le W_j^{-1/2} \quad\text{for almost every }y\in K_*.$$ The partitions may differ between the four roles. Then $$\sum_{K\in\mathcal K}\|K\| \bigl\|\mathcal H_{K,S}((z_{j,s(K)})_{j\in S})\bigr\| \le C(c_0,C_0)\|K_*\|. \label{eq:ll-depth}$$ The constant is independent of the tree, kernels, depth range, and block partitions. It can be chosen uniformly over the four sets $S$.
  - Explanation: For each of the four role sets formed by zero and a three-element subset, a depth-varying local bound holds on dyadic trees, with a constant independent of the tree, kernels and block partitions.
- **prop-ll-oscillation** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-ll-oscillation)): Let $(Y,\nu,R)$ be an invertible probability preserving system, let $J\subset\{1,2,3,4\}$ have three elements, and let the functions $g_j:Y\to\mathbb C$, $j\in J$, be measurable with $\|g_j\|\le1$. For an averaging profile set $$V_k^\varphi(y)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}\varphi_{2^k}(n) \prod_{j\in J}g_j(R^{jn}y),\qquad k\ge0.$$ There is $C_\varphi<\infty$ such that, for all deterministic integers $0\le k_1<\cdots<k_{W+1}$, $$\sum_{b=1}^W\int_Y \max_{k_b\le\ell\le k_{b+1}} \|V_\ell^\varphi-V_{k_b}^\varphi\|\,\,d\nu \le C_\varphi\sqrt W. \label{eq:ll-oscillation}$$ The constant is independent of the system, inputs, endpoints, and $W$.
  - Explanation: For every three-element subset of the four times, smoothed orbit averages satisfy a square-root oscillation bound over deterministic scale blocks, uniformly in the system and inputs.

        "exact_quote": "The following statements hold for standard probability-preserving systems.*\n\n1.  *There is a maximal measure-distal factor $W_Y$. Every measure-preserving automorphism commuting with $R$ preserves $W_Y$.*\n\n2.  *Factors and joinings of distal systems are distal. A distal system is relatively distal over each of its factors. For every nonzero integer $q$, $R$ is distal if and only if $R^q$ is distal.*\n\n3.  *Relative weak mixing passes to nonzero integer powers and to conditionally independent base change. More generally, the product of relatively weakly mixing fibre laws over any invariant joining of their base laws is relatively weakly mixing over that joining.*\n\n4.  *A relatively weakly mixing extension and a relatively distal extension of the same base are relatively disjoint: the only invariant joining which identifies their base coordinates is the relatively independent joining.*\n\n5.  *If $R$ is distal, then, for every $d\\ge1$ and bounded $g_1,\\ldots,g_d$, the averages $$\\frac1N\\sum_{n=1}^N\\prod_{i=1}^d g_i(R^{in}y)$$ converge almost surely.*\n\n*If $(S_Y^t)_{t\\in\\mathbb R}$ is a probability-preserving flow, the maximal distal factor $W_Y$ of $S_Y^1$ is a flow factor.       "exact_statement": "Formula <a href=\"#eq:selected-law\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:selected-law\">[eq:selected-law]</a> defines a probability measure $\\lambda_Y$ on $Y^I$ with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Every marginal is $m_Y$, and $\\lambda_Y$ is invariant under $(y_i)_i\\mapsto(S_Y^{it}y_i)_i$ for every $t\\in\\mathbb R$. If $\\theta:Y'\\to Y$ is an extension over $C$, then $(\\theta^I)_*\\lambda_{Y'}=\\lambda_Y$. These projections also hold for countable inverse limits of extensions.*\n\n\n2.  *The law $\\Lambda_Y$ of $P$ is its deterministic-length law: for bounded $H_i$ on $W_Y$, $$\\label{eq:ordinary-parameter-law}\n     \\int\\prod_iH_i(w_i)\\,\\,d\\Lambda_Y\n     =\\lim_{L\\to\\infty}\\int_Y\\frac1L\\int_0^L\n           \\prod_i H_i(\\omega_Y(S_Y^{it}y))\\,\\,dt\\,\\,dm_Y(y).$$*\n\n\n3.  *For every proper subset $J\\subsetneq I$, the conditional law of $(y_i)_{i\\in J}$ given the *entire* parameter array $P$ is $$\\label{eq:proper-fibres}\n     \\operatorname{Law}_{\\lambda_Y}\\bigl((y_i)_{i\\in J}\\mid P\\bigr)\n     =\\bigotimes_{i\\in J}m_{Y,P_i}.$$*\n\n*In a countable inverse limit, functions pulled back from the selected laws at finite stages are dense in the $L^2$ space of the limiting selected law.",
      "explanation": "The ultrafilter limit along selected lengths defines a probability law on four outputs with the original marginals, flow invariance and functoriality under extensions. Its parameter law is the deterministic-length law, and proper sub-tuples are conditionally product measures given the whole distal parameter array.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-packing",
        "exact_quote": "There is a universal constant $C$ with the following property. Let $0<\\varepsilon\\le1$, $d\\ge1$, and $L\\ge0$ be given, with $d,L$ integers. Let $x_1,\\ldots,x_m$ and $y_1,\\ldots,y_m$ lie in the unit balls of real Hilbert spaces $H_1,H_2$. For each family suppose that a graph on $\\{1,\\ldots,m\\}$ of maximum degree at most $L$ contains every off-diagonal pair whose absolute inner product exceeds $\\eta$, where $$0\\le\\eta\\le\\varepsilon^2/10^4.$$ The two exceptional graphs may differ. For every orthogonal projection $P$ on $H_1\\otimes H_2$ of rank at most $d$, $$\\label{eq:packing-quantitative}\n \\#\\{n:\\lVert P(x_n\\otimes y_n)\\rVert\\ge\\varepsilon\\}\n \\le \\frac{C(1+L)}{\\varepsilon^2}\n       \\exp\\!\\left(C\\varepsilon^{-2}\\sqrt d\\log(2+d)\\right).$$ In particular, for $$\\label{eq:packing-dimension}\n                  d_m=\\left\\lceil(1+\\log m)^{3/2}\\right\\rceil,$$ the left side is $o_{\\varepsilon,L}(m)$, uniformly over the Hilbert spaces, the vectors, the projections, and the allowed values of $\\eta$.",
        "source_id": "PM-154.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-packing",
          "exact_quote": "There is a universal constant $C$ with the following property. Let $0<\\varepsilon\\le1$, $d\\ge1$, and $L\\ge0$ be given, with $d,L$ integers. Let $x_1,\\ldots,x_m$ and $y_1,\\ldots,y_m$ lie in the unit balls of real Hilbert spaces $H_1,H_2$. For each family suppose that a graph on $\\{1,\\ldots,m\\}$ of maximum degree at most $L$ contains every off-diagonal pair whose absolute inner product exceeds $\\eta$, where $$0\\le\\eta\\le\\varepsilon^2/10^4.$$ The two exceptional graphs may differ. For every orthogonal projection $P$ on $H_1\\otimes H_2$ of rank at most $d$, $$\\label{eq:packing-quantitative}\n \\#\\{n:\\lVert P(x_n\\otimes y_n)\\rVert\\ge\\varepsilon\\}\n \\le \\frac{C(1+L)}{\\varepsilon^2}\n       \\exp\\!\\left(C\\varepsilon^{-2}\\sqrt d\\log(2+d)\\right).$$ In particular, for $$\\label{eq:packing-dimension}\n                  d_m=\\left\\lceil(1+\\log m)^{3/2}\\right\\rceil,$$ the left side is $o_{\\varepsilon,L}(m)$, uniformly over the Hilbert spaces, the vectors, the projections, and the allowed values of $\\eta$.",
          "source_id": "PM-154.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There is a universal constant $C$ with the following property. Let $0<\\varepsilon\\le1$, $d\\ge1$, and $L\\ge0$ be given, with $d,L$ integers. For every orthogonal projection $P$ on $H_1\\otimes H_2$ of rank at most $d$, $$\\label{eq:packing-quantitative}\n \\#\\{n:\\lVert P(x_n\\otimes y_n)\\rVert\\ge\\varepsilon\\}\n \\le \\frac{C(1+L)}{\\varepsilon^2}\n       \\exp\\!\\left(C\\varepsilon^{-2}\\sqrt d\\log(2+d)\\right).$$ In particular, for $$\\label{eq:packing-dimension}\n                  d_m=\\left\\lceil(1+\\log m)^{3/2}\\right\\rceil,$$ the left side is $o_{\\varepsilon,L}(m)$, uniformly over the Hilbert spaces, the vectors, the projections, and the allowed values of $\\eta$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-packing-gaussian",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B_1,\\ldots,B_r$ be matrices between two finite-dimensional real Hilbert spaces, and put $$H_j=\\begin{pmatrix}0&B_j\\\\ B_j^*&0\\end{pmatrix},\n \\qquad S=\\sum_{j=1}^r H_j^2.$$ If $\\lVert S\\rVert_{\\rm op}\\le\\sqrt d$ and $\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}S\\le2d$ for some $d\\ge1$, then for independent standard normal variables $g_j$, $$\\label{eq:packing-gaussian-bound}\n       \\mathbb E\\left\\|\\sum_{j=1}^r g_jB_j\\right\\|_{\\rm op}\n             \\le C d^{1/4}\\sqrt{\\log(2+d)}.$$ The constant does not depend on the dimensions of the two spaces.",
        "source_id": "PM-154.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-packing-gaussian",
          "exact_quote": "Let $B_1,\\ldots,B_r$ be matrices between two finite-dimensional real Hilbert spaces, and put $$H_j=\\begin{pmatrix}0&B_j\\\\ B_j^*&0\\end{pmatrix},\n \\qquad S=\\sum_{j=1}^r H_j^2.$$ If $\\lVert S\\rVert_{\\rm op}\\le\\sqrt d$ and $\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}S\\le2d$ for some $d\\ge1$, then for independent standard normal variables $g_j$, $$\\label{eq:packing-gaussian-bound}\n       \\mathbb E\\left\\|\\sum_{j=1}^r g_jB_j\\right\\|_{\\rm op}\n             \\le C d^{1/4}\\sqrt{\\log(2+d)}.$$ The constant does not depend on the dimensions of the two spaces.",
          "source_id": "PM-154.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $B_1,\\ldots,B_r$ be matrices between two finite-dimensional real Hilbert spaces, and put $$H_j=\\begin{pmatrix}0&B_j\\\\ B_j^*&0\\end{pmatrix},\n \\qquad S=\\sum_{j=1}^r H_j^2.$$ If $\\lVert S\\rVert_{\\rm op}\\le\\sqrt d$ and $\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}S\\le2d$ for some $d\\ge1$, then for independent standard normal variables $g_j$, $$\\label{eq:packing-gaussian-bound}\n       \\mathbb E\\left\\|\\sum_{j=1}^r g_jB_j\\right\\|_{\\rm op}\n             \\le C d^{1/4}\\sqrt{\\log(2+d)}.$$ The constant does not depend on the dimensions of the two spaces.",
      "explanation": "If the quadratic sum of the block matrices has controlled operator norm and trace, the expected operator norm of the Gaussian combination is bounded independently of the dimensions of the two spaces.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **mixing transformation** ([source](#eq:mixing)): We say that $T$ is mixing if $$\label{eq:mixing} \mu(A\cap T^{-n}B)\longrightarrow\mu(A)\mu(B) \qquad (\|n\|\longrightarrow\infty)$$ for every $A,B\in\mathcal F$.
- **suspension coordinates** ([source](#pm-h-10)): Write $\xi:C\to X$ for its spatial coordinate and $\upsilon:C\to\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ for its phase.
- **relatively compact extension** ([source](#pm-h-8)): An extension is *relatively compact* if its $L^2$ space is generated by these invariant modules.
- **relatively distal extension** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A *relatively distal* extension is a tower of relatively compact extensions and inverse limits.
- **distal system without base** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Distality without a specified base means distality over the trivial factor.
- **factor of a flow with speed** ([source](#sec:channels)): A factor of a flow $Y$ with values in $C$ and speed $q>0$ means a measure-preserving map $D:Y\to C$ satisfying $D(S_Y^t y)=S_C^{qt}D(y)$ for every $t$, modulo the usual null sets.
- **channel** ([source](#stmt-def-channel)): A *channel* is a flow extension $\pi_Y:(Y,m_Y,S_Y^t)\to C$ together with a finite list of output factors $D_\alpha:Y\to C$, of positive rational speeds $q_\alpha$, partitioned into three groups $\mathcal D_1,\mathcal D_2,\mathcal D_3$.
- **output roles** ([source](#pm-h-15)): We call the indices in $A$ the *output roles*.
- **role set** ([source](#pm-h-21)): For this appendix, a *role set* is a set $S$ of four distinct integer coefficients.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-packing-eta-threshold | exceptional inner-product threshold | ≤ | $\varepsilon^2/10^4$ | accuracy parameter in the unit interval |
| q-packing-dimension | testing-subspace dimension | = | $\left\lceil(1+\log m)^{3/2}\right\rceil$ | index m |
| q-gaussian-expected-norm | expected operator norm of Gaussian matrix sum | ≤ | $C d^{1/4}\sqrt{\log(2+d)}$ | d at least one; constant independent of dimensions |
| q-residual-gram-bound | residual tensor average squared norm | ≤ | $\frac1{d_m}$ | per length class |
| q-role-count | maximal number of output roles | ≤ | $2$ | role number r |
| q-support-width | support width in the difference variable | ≤ | $\|K\|/3$ | dyadic interval K |
| q-offset-area | area of the offset domain | = | $\frac{4e^2}{s_+-s_-}$ | role set S and small radius e |
| q-oscillation-bound | integrated oscillation over W scale blocks | ≤ | $C_\varphi\sqrt W$ | deterministic endpoints and number of blocks W |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-ll-depth** [claim](#stmt-prop-ll-depth) / [proof](#pm-h-25): Check how each coefficient-dependent step of the imported local construction changes for the four role sets, then rerun the depth-local scheduling. -> uniform depth-varying local bound for the four role sets
- **step-ll-oscillation** [claim](#stmt-prop-ll-oscillation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-ll-oscillation): Build smooth kernels from a profile difference, transfer the local bound to integer sequences and then to orbits by Calderón transference. -> square-root oscillation bound for averages over each triple
- **step-lower-length** [claim](#stmt-lem-lower-length) / [proof](#subsec:ll-convergence): Oscillation bound gives almost-everywhere Cauchy property along geometric lengths; profile approximation and norm convergence identify the limit; interpolate to all lengths. -> pointwise convergence of proper-subset averages
- **step-structure** [claim](#stmt-prop-structure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-structure): Use the Furstenberg–Zimmer structure theory with module arguments for joinings, powers, base change, disjointness and a disintegration argument for the pointwise assertion. -> maximal distal factor and its closure properties
- **step-relative-norm** [claim](#stmt-lem-relative-norm) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-relative-norm): Induction on the number of functions with the van der Corput inequality and the relative weak mixing correlations. -> relatively weakly mixing characteristic factor
- **step-selectors** [claim](#stmt-prop-selectors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-selectors): Reduce to centred real functions, choose measurable selectors on a finite grid of lengths, and insert a phase test comparing the continuous-time average with the discrete one. -> selectors and a positive selected correlation
- **step-selected-laws** [claim](#stmt-prop-selected) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-selected): Take an ultrafilter limit of selected averages, then identify parameter and proper-slot laws using distal pointwise convergence and relative disjointness. -> selected four-output law with conditional product structure
- **step-parameter-distal** [claim](#stmt-lem-parameter-distal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-parameter-distal): Observe that each slot parameter is distal, so the joining is distal; induct over iterations. -> distal parameter arrays
- **step-packing-gaussian** [claim](#stmt-lem-packing-gaussian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-packing-gaussian): Gaussian integration by parts gives a moment recursion for traces; take a root and choose the moment order comparable to the logarithm. -> dimension-free Gaussian matrix estimate
- **step-packing** [claim](#stmt-thm-packing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-packing-gaussian-2): Discard indices with large projection onto high-variance directions, cover and cluster, bound a separated set by a Gaussian comparison and the matrix estimate. -> tensor packing bound
- **step-channel-distal** [claim](#stmt-lem-channel-distal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-channel-distal): Phases belong to the maximal distal factor; clear denominators and apply relative disjointness. -> channel identities over the maximal distal factor
- **step-channel-closure** [claim](#stmt-prop-channel-closure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-channel-closure): Proper collections use mixing of all orders; for all three groups, split by length class and combine tensor packing with a residual Gram estimate. -> channel closure under selected laws
- **step-channel-array** [claim](#stmt-cor-channel-array-distal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-channel-array-distal): Place the old parameter array in the new maximal distal factor and apply the channel conditioning lemma. -> conditional product law over old parameters
- **step-role-bound** [claim](#stmt-prop-role-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-role-bound): Four roles vanish by independence; for three roles, iterate the selected-law flow and apply Bessel's inequality to orthogonal coordinate-line products. -> at most two output roles
- **step-saturation** [claim](#stmt-prop-saturation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-saturation): Build an inverse limit of near-maximal energy extensions and pass to all tests by density. -> saturated extension
- **step-regrouped-channel** [claim](#stmt-lem-regrouped-channel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-regrouped-channel): Prove the full product law by expanding into means and centred parts and invoking maximality of the role number; proper collections follow from the old channel rule. -> channel structure on the regrouped flow
- **step-square-conditional** [claim](#stmt-lem-square-conditional) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-square-conditional): Apply saturation to the extension and use conditional independence of rows given the parameters. -> column law and row locality
- **step-reanchoring** [claim](#stmt-lem-parameter-reanchoring) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-parameter-reanchoring): Use the deterministic-length parameter law, substitute a time shift, and conclude by a monotone class argument. -> conditional independence given the corner parameter
- **step-hilbert-vectors** [claim](#stmt-lem-independent-hilbert-vectors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-independent-hilbert-vectors): Cover by small balls and use the polarization identity to get a positive inner product. -> vanishing of self-orthogonal random vectors
- **step-activation** [claim](#stmt-prop-activation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-activation): Show the square correlation is nonzero via conditional independence, a Hilbert-valued tower identity and the vector lemma, then read off the roles. -> nonzero correlation with an additional role
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-activation-2): Assume failure, take a correlation with the maximal number of roles, use the role bound, saturation and activation to contradict maximality. -> pointwise convergence to the product of integrals

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-cancellation-vector**: For each admissible four-element role set, the primitive integer coefficient vector proportional to the inverse products of role differences has all entries nonzero and annihilates the polynomials 1, u and u squared, i.e. the three displayed moment sums vanish.
- **inv-nonzero-coefficients**: Every coefficient of the cancellation vector is nonzero.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-table-as-main-proof**: Treat the finite coefficient computation as evidence for the pointwise convergence theorem itself. -> The computation only checks algebraic cancellation identities for role labels; the analytic estimates and the structural argument carry the theorem.
- **anti-translation-labels**: Assume the cancellation vector keeps a fixed shape under any change of role labels. -> The vector depends on the role set; only the moment identities are preserved by translation of labels.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The paper's main results are existence and convergence statements without a finite procedure, but the appendix gives an explicit finite construction: the primitive integer cancellation vector for each four-element role set, with exact moment identities. Only that construction is implemented and checked.

```python
# INPUTS: one line with four distinct integers, the role labels of S
# PRECONDITIONS: the four integers are pairwise distinct
# OUTPUTS: one line with four integers, the primitive cancellation vector in input order, first entry negative
# TIME: O(1) for four roles
# SPACE: O(1)
# ARITHMETIC: exact rational and integer arithmetic only
# FAILURE: repeated roles or malformed input make the program fail with an exception
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
from math import gcd
def main():
    s = [int(t) for t in sys.stdin.read().split()]
    w = []
    for j in s:
        p = 1
        for l in s:
            if l != j:
                p *= (j - l)
        w.append(Fraction(1, p))
    L = 1
    for x in w:
        L = L * x.denominator // gcd(L, x.denominator)
    c = [int(x * L) for x in w]
    g = 0
    for x in c:
        g = gcd(g, abs(x))
    c = [x // g for x in c]
    if c[0] > 0:
        c = [-x for x in c]
    print(" ".join(str(x) for x in c))
main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an existence and convergence theorem by contradiction. It gives no procedure that code could execute, and the argument is non-constructive. It offers a program no inputs to run on. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result concerns limits along all lengths for fixed bounded functions. A simulation runs only finitely many steps, and the theorem states no convergence rate, so it cannot predict what a finite run does. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No measurable quantity, checker or analyser can be derived from the argument. The key objects are selected laws taken along an ultrafilter limit, and these cannot be computed. No tool contract follows. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof argues by contradiction, using a maximal number of output roles and saturated extensions built by a limit procedure. These are not finite algorithmic steps, and the paper states no running time. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces measure-theoretic structures (channels, selected laws, distal factors) that are not finite representations. It proposes no storage or indexing scheme. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only finite-dimensional estimate, tensor packing, is a deterministic inequality used inside a proof. The paper gives no kernel or dataflow to accelerate, so nothing maps to hardware. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The mixing and independence statements are asymptotic and rate-free. They give no concrete security bound or randomness guarantee, so they support no cryptographic claim. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, sample complexity or generalisation statement appears. The averages are fixed observables along an orbit of a single transformation, and no training objective is involved. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective, constraint set or optimisation algorithm is present. The energy maximisation in the saturation step is an existence device for extensions, not a solver. It yields no optimum to compute. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no protocol, communication model or fault model. The theorem does not constrain the behaviour of any distributed process. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The contribution is a pure theorem in ergodic theory. It needs strong mixing and fixed bounded inputs, and it concludes only asymptotic almost-everywhere convergence. It offers no direct engineering transfer. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-mixing-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The product-of-integrals limit is proved only for invertible, bimeasurable, measure-preserving transformations that are mixing. For non-mixing systems the limit need not be the product of the integrals. Blocked use: ['Applying the fourfold product limit to periodic, non-mixing or merely ergodic systems']
- **lim-no-rate** (approximation): The result is qualitative. No mixing rate is assumed and none is produced, so there is no bound on how fast the averages approach the product of the integrals. Blocked use: ['Predicting how close a finite-length average is to the product of the means', 'Choosing a sample size or stopping time from the theorem']
- **lim-fixed-functions** (hypothesis): The bounded functions are fixed before the limit is taken. The exceptional null set and the convergence behaviour depend on the chosen functions, so nothing is uniform over a class of observables. Blocked use: ['Claiming uniform convergence over all bounded observables', 'Letting the observables vary with the averaging length']
- **lim-lower-length-scope** (formal_scope): The convergence of proper-subset averages is stated on a standard probability space for invertible transformations, with no mixing or ergodicity assumed. It covers only proper subsets of the four times, not all four. Blocked use: ['Reading the lemma as pointwise convergence of the full fourfold average on an arbitrary system']
- **lim-structure-standard** (formal_scope): The structure statements on maximal distal factors, powers, joinings and disjointness are formulated for standard probability-preserving systems. Blocked use: ['Using the structure theory on non-standard spaces without first passing to a standard factor']
- **lim-selector-contrapositive** (nonconstructive): The selectors come from assuming that the main theorem fails. They are conditional objects for a proof by contradiction and are not produced by any procedure. Blocked use: ['Treating the selectors or selected laws as computable objects', 'Using the selected length as an algorithmic stopping rule']
- **lim-packing-regime** (hypothesis): The tensor packing bound requires unit-ball vectors whose large inner products lie on a graph of bounded degree, with the small-pair threshold tied to the accuracy parameter. It carries an unspecified universal constant, so it is not a numerical estimate. Blocked use: ['Using the bound with unbounded exceptional degree', 'Reading off a numerical count from the unspecified constant']
- **lim-gaussian-constant** (approximation): The Gaussian matrix estimate requires controlled operator norm and trace of the quadratic sum. Its constant is not specified, so the bound is dimension-free but not numerically explicit. Blocked use: ['Quoting a numerical value for the expected operator norm']
- **lim-channel-partial-independence** (edge_case): A channel only requires independence of the original coordinate from proper collections of the three groups, not from all three jointly. Empty groups are allowed. Blocked use: ['Assuming full independence of the original coordinate from all three output groups']
- **lim-role-bound** (counterexample_scope): The bound of at most two output roles holds under the supposition that pointwise convergence fails. It is an intermediate step of the contradiction, not an unconditional fact about correlations. Blocked use: ['Quoting the role bound outside the proof by contradiction']
- **lim-saturation-existence** (nonconstructive): The saturated extension is obtained from near-maximal energy choices along a limit, so it is an existence statement without an explicit construction. Blocked use: ['Implementing the saturated extension as an algorithm']
- **lim-oscillation-constant** (approximation): The oscillation estimate has a constant depending on the averaging profile but not on the system, inputs, endpoints or number of blocks. The constant is not explicit. Blocked use: ['Deriving a quantitative convergence rate from the oscillation bound']
- **lim-depth-bound-imported** (formal_scope): The depth-varying local bound is proved by modifying cited analytic constructions for the four role sets. It is only as strong as those imported estimates and has an unspecified constant. Blocked use: ['Claiming the local bound for role sets other than the four considered']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that fourfold ergodic averages along the times $`n,2n,3n,4n`$ converge almost everywhere to the product of the integrals for every invertible, bimeasurable, mixing probability-preserving transformation and every fixed choice of bounded measurable inputs. Convergence holds along all positive integer averaging lengths. No quantitative mixing rate is assumed, and the probability space need not be standard.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:mixing"></a>
Let $(X,\mathcal F,\mu,T)$ be an invertible probability-preserving system. For bounded measurable functions $f_1,\ldots,f_d$, the multiple ergodic averages are $$A_N(f_1,\ldots,f_d)(x)
=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\prod_{j=1}^d f_j(T^{jn}x).$$ The general pointwise question asks whether these averages converge almost everywhere for every finite $d$ on every invertible probability-preserving system. Here we treat four functions under the mixing hypothesis. We say that $T$ is mixing if $$\label{eq:mixing}
 \mu(A\cap T^{-n}B)\longrightarrow\mu(A)\mu(B)
 \qquad (|n|\longrightarrow\infty)$$ for every $A,B\in\mathcal F$. The functions are fixed throughout each convergence assertion. No rate in <a href="#eq:mixing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixing">[eq:mixing]</a> is assumed.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $(X,\mathcal F,\mu)$ be a probability space and let $T:X\to X$ be invertible, bimeasurable, measure preserving, and mixing in the sense of <a href="#eq:mixing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixing">[eq:mixing]</a>. For every $f_1,f_2,f_3,f_4\in L^\infty(\mu)$, $$\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N
 f_1(T^nx)f_2(T^{2n}x)f_3(T^{3n}x)f_4(T^{4n}x)
 \longrightarrow \prod_{j=1}^4\int_X f_j\,d\mu$$ for $\mu$-almost every $x$, along all positive integers $N$. The original probability space need not be standard.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and inputs

Birkhoff’s pointwise ergodic theorem treats a single observation along an orbit <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Birkhoff](#ref-Birkhoff)]. Furstenberg’s ergodic proof of Szemerédi’s theorem made products at arithmetic-progression times a central object of study <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Furstenberg](#ref-Furstenberg)]. For weakly mixing transformations, these averages converge in $L^2$ to the product of the integrals. For arbitrary transformations, Host and Kra established norm convergence of every finite consecutive length using nilpotent characteristic factors <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[HostKra](#ref-HostKra), Theorem 1.1]; Ziegler independently developed universal characteristic factors and another proof <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Ziegler](#ref-Ziegler)]. Norm convergence alone does not control the exceptional lengths at an individual point.

Bourgain proved pointwise convergence for two powers of one transformation <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Bourgain](#ref-Bourgain)]. Higher-order pointwise results include $K$ systems <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DerrienLesigne](#ref-DerrienLesigne), Theorem 4.2], weakly mixing systems with suitable spectral restrictions <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Assani](#ref-Assani)], and weakly mixing systems whose pairwise-independent finite self-joinings are all product measures <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[GutmanHuangShaoYe](#ref-GutmanHuangShaoYe)]. Huang, Shao and Ye proved pointwise convergence of every finite consecutive length on ergodic measure-distal systems <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[HuangShaoYe](#ref-HuangShaoYe), Theorem C], extending the distal investigation of Lesigne <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Lesigne](#ref-Lesigne)]. Recent pointwise theorems for polynomial iterates include the bilinear result of Krause, Mirek and Tao <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KrauseMirekTao](#ref-KrauseMirekTao)] and the distinct-degree multilinear result of Kosz, Mirek, Peluse, Wan and Wright <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[KoszMirekPeluseWanWright](#ref-KoszMirekPeluseWanWright)]. Their degree hypotheses do not cover the four linear iterates considered here. Kuca’s survey <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Kuca](#ref-Kuca)] gives a broader account of the norm and pointwise questions.

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> uses three companion results. The multiple-mixing theorem of <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MixingCompanion](#ref-MixingCompanion), Theorem 1.1] supplies mixing of every finite order from <a href="#eq:mixing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixing">[eq:mixing]</a>. The analytic construction of <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[TripleCompanion](#ref-TripleCompanion)], based on the local estimates of <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HilbertCompanion](#ref-HilbertCompanion)], supplies an oscillation estimate for threefold averages. Its oscillation assertion holds on arbitrary invertible systems; mixing is used separately to identify limits. We need the threefold estimate at each three-element subset of $\{1,2,3,4\}$. Appendix <a href="#sec:lower-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lower-length">7</a> proves the required coefficient adaptation and the resulting pointwise convergence lemma, including its nonergodic formulation. The local analytic theorems themselves are cited with their hypotheses and locations. Thus the contribution here is the fourfold deduction from these specified inputs.

The structural part uses the Furstenberg–Zimmer theory of relatively compact and relatively weakly mixing extensions. We record the nonergodic and power-stability statements at their point of use in Section <a href="#sec:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:structure">2</a>. The construction also uses extensions on which certain conditional expectations cannot improve. Such energy maximization belongs to the same general method as sated extensions <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Austin](#ref-Austin)]; the data being saturated here come from selected four-coordinate laws.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The difficulty caused by selected lengths

Suppose that the conclusion fails. After subtracting means, there are four centred real functions and a positive-measure set of points at which their averages remain positively separated from zero along arbitrarily late lengths. We choose one such length $N_a(x)$ after scale $2^a$. Averaging the configurations $$(T^nx,T^{2n}x,T^{3n}x,T^{4n}x),\qquad 1\le n\le N_a(x),$$ and passing to a limit preserves a nonzero correlation. A constant-roof suspension makes these limit laws invariant under a flow and permits the changes of speed needed later.

Selection is the main analytic obstruction. Conditioning on a length chosen from the initial state can change the joint law of independent auxiliary variables. Ordinary norm convergence, with no rate, does not by itself bound all the resulting errors. We introduce three groups of auxiliary spatial coordinates. Their joint law is a product, and any two groups together are independent of the original coordinate, after conditioning on the suspension phases. A length event may change the full three-group law, but preserves the two-group marginals. This is precisely the amount of independence used in Section <a href="#sec:channels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:channels">5</a>. For a finite model of the distinction, let $U,V,W$ be independent symmetric signs. Their product is independent of each pair, but conditioning on $UVW=1$ constrains the triple.

The estimate making this possible is a finite-dimensional packing statement proved in Section <a href="#sec:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packing">4</a>. If two bounded families of Hilbert-space vectors have small pairwise inner products outside a graph of bounded degree, few of their tensor products can have large projection onto a subspace of slowly growing dimension. A Gaussian comparison and a trace-moment estimate give a quantitative form of this assertion. Applied to the leading covariance subspace, it controls the projected part of each selected average. The complementary part has a square-summable error over the possible lengths. The estimate is independent of the ambient Hilbert-space dimensions and requires no quantitative mixing rate.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### From a correlation to a contradiction

Each auxiliary system carries both an original spatial coordinate and the three groups of additional coordinates. We call such a system a channel after specifying its exact independence conditions in Definition <a href="#def:channel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:channel">10</a>. In its selected four-coordinate law, consider nonzero correlations in which each slot uses either a centred function of the original coordinate or a centred function of the additional coordinates. Bounded functions of the distal parameters are allowed as weights. The initial failure gives a correlation with no additional-coordinate slots.

Choose a channel and a correlation maximizing the number of slots of the second kind. Independence excludes four such slots. An iterated array construction excludes three: products along different axes through a fixed corner are orthogonal, but each has the same nonzero correlation with the function at that corner. Bessel’s inequality rules this out. At most two slots can therefore be of the second kind.

The final construction increases that number. First enlarge the channel until the conditional expectations needed by the argument are stable under further extensions. Then regard one selected four-coordinate law as a new system and select once more. The result is a square of old states. Its distinguished row and column meet at one corner; saturation supplies their exact conditional laws. A reanchoring identity on the distal parameters shows that the row and column correlation cannot vanish. Regrouping the other coordinates therefore replaces one original-coordinate slot by an additional-coordinate slot, contradicting maximality.

Sections <a href="#sec:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:structure">2</a> and <a href="#sec:selected" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:selected">3</a> establish the structural inputs and selected laws. The packing estimate and channel preservation are proved in Sections <a href="#sec:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packing">4</a> and <a href="#sec:channels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:channels">5</a>. Section <a href="#sec:activation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:activation">6</a> carries out the maximality argument and completes the proof. The coefficient-dependent analytic work is collected in Appendix <a href="#sec:lower-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lower-length">7</a>.

<a id="sec:structure"></a>
### Convergence inputs and relative structure

The auxiliary systems in the proof will be extensions and joinings of a suspension of the original transformation. They need not be ergodic. We therefore formulate the structural facts in the nonergodic setting, and distinguish the pointwise input for three functions from the norm estimate available for any finite number of functions.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Standard systems and the pointwise inputs

Put $I=\{1,2,3,4\}$. It suffices to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> on a standard probability space. Indeed, for a given tuple of bounded functions, the map recording all their integer translates takes values in a countable product of compact scalar discs. The pushforward measure and the shift give a standard factor, the functions descend to its coordinate functions, and mixing passes to this factor. Almost-sure convergence there pulls back to the original space. We henceforth write $(X,\mu,T)$ for this standard mixing system. All factors and conditional expectations are understood modulo null sets; probability spaces may be completed.

We use <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MixingCompanion](#ref-MixingCompanion), Theorem 1.1] in the following form. For every fixed finite family $g_1,\ldots,g_s\in L^\infty(\mu)$, <a id="eq:all-orders-input"></a>$$\label{eq:all-orders-input}
 \int_X\prod_{j=1}^s g_j(T^{n_j}x)\,\,d\mu(x)
 \longrightarrow \prod_{j=1}^s\int_Xg_j\,\,d\mu
 \quad\text{if}\quad \min_{j\ne l}|n_j-n_l|\longrightarrow\infty.$$ The bounded-function formulation follows from the set formulation by simple-function approximation. All uses of this input involve fixed functions; no rate of mixing is assumed.

The other companion input will be used on systems without mixing. Its coefficient dependence is important, so we isolate its exact scope.

<a id="stmt-lem-lower-length"></a>
<div id="lem:lower-length" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Lower-length convergence). *Let $(Y,m,R)$ be an invertible probability-preserving transformation on a standard probability space. If $\varnothing\ne J\subsetneq I$ and $g_i\in L^\infty(m)$ for $i\in J$, then $$\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\prod_{i\in J}g_i(R^{in}y)$$ converges for $m$-almost every $y$.*

</div>

The proof, including the changes of coefficients in the analytic estimates of <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[TripleCompanion](#ref-TripleCompanion), [HilbertCompanion](#ref-HilbertCompanion)], is given in Appendix <a href="#sec:lower-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lower-length">7</a>. Neither ergodicity nor mixing is an assumption of Lemma <a href="#lem:lower-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lower-length">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Distal factors and relatively weakly mixing extensions

<a id="eq:rwm-correlations"></a>
Let $(Y,m,R)$ be a standard probability-preserving system, and let $B$ be a factor. We use the conditional-correlation characterization of *relative weak mixing*: for bounded $f,g$, with $\mathbb E[f\mid B]=0$, $$\label{eq:rwm-correlations}
 \frac1H\sum_{h=1}^H
 \bigl\|\mathbb E[\overline f\,(g\circ R^h)\mid B]\bigr\|_2^2
 \longrightarrow0.$$ For $F_1,\ldots,F_r\in L^2(m)$, the associated finitely generated $L^\infty(B)$-module is $$\left\{\sum_{j=1}^r a_j F_j:a_j\in L^\infty(B)\right\}.$$ It is invariant if composition with $R$ and with $R^{-1}$ preserves this space. An extension is *relatively compact* if its $L^2$ space is generated by these invariant modules. The individual modules need not be closed in $L^2$; closure is taken after their union is spanned. This is the compact-module characterization in its corrected form <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Jamneshan](#ref-Jamneshan), Theorem 4.1]<a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[JamneshanCorrigendum](#ref-JamneshanCorrigendum), Theorem 2.5]. A *relatively distal* extension is a tower of relatively compact extensions and inverse limits. Distality without a specified base means distality over the trivial factor. These definitions include nonergodic systems; in particular an identity action is compact.

<a id="stmt-prop-structure"></a>
<div id="prop:structure" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Relative structure). *The following statements hold for standard probability-preserving systems.*

1.  *There is a maximal measure-distal factor $W_Y$. It contains the invariant factor, and $Y\to W_Y$ is relatively weakly mixing. A distal factor pulls back into the maximal distal factor of any extension. Every measure-preserving automorphism commuting with $R$ preserves $W_Y$.*

2.  *Factors and joinings of distal systems are distal. A distal system is relatively distal over each of its factors. For every nonzero integer $q$, $R$ is distal if and only if $R^q$ is distal.*

3.  *Relative weak mixing passes to nonzero integer powers and to conditionally independent base change. More generally, the product of relatively weakly mixing fibre laws over any invariant joining of their base laws is relatively weakly mixing over that joining.*

4.  *A relatively weakly mixing extension and a relatively distal extension of the same base are relatively disjoint: the only invariant joining which identifies their base coordinates is the relatively independent joining.*

5.  *If $R$ is distal, then, for every $d\ge1$ and bounded $g_1,\ldots,g_d$, the averages $$\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\prod_{i=1}^d g_i(R^{in}y)$$ converge almost surely.*

*If $(S_Y^t)_{t\in\mathbb R}$ is a probability-preserving flow, the maximal distal factor $W_Y$ of $S_Y^1$ is a flow factor. Its action at every nonzero rational time is distal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-structure"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-structure</summary>

*Proof.* The existence assertion and the relatively weakly mixing extension are the Furstenberg–Zimmer structure theorem <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Furstenberg](#ref-Furstenberg), [Zimmer](#ref-Zimmer)]; its nonergodic probability-algebra formulation is given in <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Jamneshan](#ref-Jamneshan), Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]. We recall the consequences needed here, with particular attention to changes of base and of time.

At a compact stage, an invariant finitely generated module remains such a module after the base is enlarged. One may use bounded generators: for a vector of generators transforming by unitary matrices over the base, truncate by its invariant pointwise length. Products of bounded generators lie in finitely generated tensor modules. Consequently the join of compact stages is compact over the joined base, and the same assertion for distal towers follows by passing to closures at limit stages. This proves closure under joinings and the assertion about enlarging the base to any factor of a distal system. It also shows that distal factors of a system lie in its maximal distal factor. Applying this observation to an automorphism commuting with $R$, and then to its inverse, shows that it preserves $W_Y$. The invariant factor is compact and therefore belongs to $W_Y$.

Compact modules for $R$ are compact modules for $R^q$, so distality passes to powers. Conversely, suppose $q>0$ and $R^q$ is distal. Its canonical compact tower is preserved by $R$, which commutes with $R^q$. At each successor stage replace an $R^q$-invariant finitely generated module by the sum of its first $q$ translates under $R$. This is a finitely generated $R$-invariant module over the same base. The resulting tower proves distality for $R$. Negative powers give the same conclusion by inversion.

For relative weak mixing, the nonnegative summands in <a href="#eq:rwm-correlations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rwm-correlations">[eq:rwm-correlations]</a> show that restriction to multiples of a fixed positive integer still has Cesàro limit zero. Invariance and complex conjugation handle negative powers. To verify the assertion about joined bases, let $Y_j\to B_j$ be the extensions and let $\sigma$ be an invariant joining of the base measures. On the resulting space give the fibres the law $$\int\bigotimes_j m_j(\,dy_j\mid b_j)\,\,d\sigma((b_j)_j).$$ For bounded tensor tests, their conditional correlation over the full base has the form <a id="eq:product-rwm-correlation"></a>$$\label{eq:product-rwm-correlation}
 a(b)\,c(S_B^h b)
 \prod_j\mathbb E[\overline{f_j}\,(g_j\circ R_j^h)\mid B_j](b_j).$$ If one $f_j$ is conditionally centred, the squared $L^2(\sigma)$ norm is bounded by a constant times the squared norm of that factor in $L^2(B_j)$. Here only the $j$th marginal of $\sigma$ is used. Its Cesàro mean tends to zero. Subtracting the conditional expectation of a tensor and telescoping shows that such centred tensors span a dense subspace of the orthocomplement of the full base. Bounded approximation now proves the claim. The same calculation with an arbitrary extra base coordinate proves stability under conditionally independent base change.

For relative disjointness we argue with conditional modules. The absolute weak-mixing disjointness principle goes back to Furstenberg <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[FurstenbergDisjointness](#ref-FurstenbergDisjointness), Part I, Section 7]. First take a compact extension of the base. A joining defines a base-linear, time-intertwining conditional expectation operator from its $L^2$ space to the relatively weakly mixing side. It takes each finitely generated invariant module into another such module. Relative weak mixing says that the latter lies in the base, so the joining is relatively independent. Induct along a distal tower: once independence has been proved at a stage, the extension on the other side is the conditionally independent base change of the original relatively weakly mixing extension. The preceding calculation allows the compact-stage argument to be repeated. Conditional expectations pass to the limit at limit stages. This proves relative disjointness without an ergodicity assumption. It also gives closure of distal systems under factors: if $D$ is distal and $B$ is its factor, then $D$ is distal over the pullback of $W_B$; relative disjointness of $D\to W_B$ and $B\to W_B$ forces the latter extension to be trivial.

For the pointwise assertion, the ergodic case is <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[HuangShaoYe](#ref-HuangShaoYe), Theorem C], whose distality is measure-theoretic. For a nonergodic distal system, start its compact tower with the invariant factor and disintegrate over that factor. The tower is countable on a standard space: its genuinely increasing successor stages give nonzero orthogonal differences in a separable $L^2$ space. At each stage choose a countable generating algebra. Approximate each of its indicators by functions in the compact modules at a successor stage, or in the preceding factors at a limit stage, with summable squared $L^2$ errors. Fubini makes these errors summable in the conditional $L^2$ spaces on almost every ergodic component, simultaneously for all the chosen approximations. The restricted generating algebras are dense for every component measure. The countably many identities expressing invariance of the chosen modules also disintegrate. Thus the successor stages remain compact and the limit stages remain generated by their predecessors on a common conull set of components. Every intermediate factor has the same invariant algebra, so each component tower consists of ergodic systems; its compact extensions are isometric by the compact-extension structure theorem. Each component is thus an ergodic distal system. The cited theorem applies to the given tuple on almost every component, proving the assertion.

Finally, every flow time commutes with time one and therefore preserves $W_Y$. If $r=p/q\ne0$ is rational, the $q$th power of its action on $W_Y$ is the distal action at integer time $p$. The power assertion gives distality at time $r$. ◻

</details>

The following norm estimate explains why proper subaverages have a particularly simple law over a distal factor. Its proof uses relative weak mixing alone.

<a id="stmt-lem-relative-norm"></a>
<div id="lem:relative-norm" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:relative-norm"></a>
**Lemma 4** (A relatively weakly mixing characteristic factor). *Suppose $Y\to B$ is relatively weakly mixing for an invertible transformation $R$. If $a_1,\ldots,a_d$ are distinct nonzero integers and $g_1,\ldots,g_d$ are bounded, then $$\label{eq:relative-norm}
 \left\|\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N
 \left(\prod_{i=1}^d g_i\circ R^{a_i n}
 -\prod_{i=1}^d\mathbb E[g_i\mid B]\circ R^{a_i n}\right)\right\|_2
 \longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-relative-norm"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-relative-norm</summary>

*Proof.* By telescoping and scaling, it suffices to consider functions bounded by one, one of which, say $g_s$, has conditional mean zero. We induct on $d$, with the empty product as the initial case. Set $v_n=\prod_i g_i\circ R^{a_i n}$ and, for a fixed $h\ge1$, put $F_{i,h}=\overline{g_i}(g_i\circ R^{a_i h})$. Invariance gives $$\langle v_n,v_{n+h}\rangle
 =\int F_{s,h}\prod_{i\ne s}
       F_{i,h}\circ R^{(a_i-a_s)n}\,\,dm.$$ The other coefficients are distinct and nonzero. The induction hypothesis replaces their factors, in the average over $n$, by their conditional expectations over $B$, with an error tending to zero. Those remaining factors are $B$-measurable and bounded by one, so $$\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \left|\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\langle v_n,v_{n+h}\rangle\right|
 \le \|\mathbb E[F_{s,h}\mid B]\|_1.$$ For $d=1$ the same inequality holds directly. The Hilbert-space van der Corput inequality bounds the squared norm of the averages of $v_n$ by an absolute constant times $$\frac1H+\frac1H\sum_{h=1}^H\|\mathbb E[F_{s,h}\mid B]\|_1$$ in the limit superior as $N\to\infty$. The second term tends to zero by <a href="#eq:rwm-correlations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rwm-correlations">[eq:rwm-correlations]</a> for $R^{a_s}$ and Cauchy–Schwarz. Letting $H\to\infty$ proves the induction. ◻

</details>

In particular, weak mixing of $T$ gives $L^2$ convergence to the product of the means for all finite distinct nonzero linear powers on $X$. In a flow, with $B$ preserved by every time, the analogue of <a href="#eq:relative-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relative-norm">[eq:relative-norm]</a> for deterministic continuous-time averages follows by writing $t=n+s$, $0\le s<1$. Apply the lemma for each fixed $s$ to $g_i\circ S_Y^{a_i s}$, and integrate the resulting $L^2$ estimate in $s$. Conditional expectation commutes with these time shifts. Bounded convergence and the final interval of length less than one complete the passage to continuous time.

<a id="sec:selected"></a>
### Selected laws and their distal parameters

A failure of pointwise convergence allows the averaging length to depend on the initial state. We encode this failure in a probability law on four outputs. The selection can change their joint law, but the convergence results of Section <a href="#sec:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:structure">2</a> will determine every proper marginal, even after all distal parameters are given.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### From failure to a sequence of selectors

Let $C=X\times[0,1)$ have measure $m_C=\mu\otimes\,du$ and the roof-one suspension flow <a id="eq:suspension"></a>$$\label{eq:suspension}
 S_C^t(x,u)=\bigl(T^{\lfloor u+t\rfloor}x,\{u+t\}\bigr).$$ Write $\xi:C\to X$ for its spatial coordinate and $\upsilon:C\to\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ for its phase. The use of a flow will let us normalize the action at any one of the four outputs.

<a id="stmt-prop-selectors"></a>
<div id="prop:selectors" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:selected-positive"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Selectors witnessing failure). *If Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> fails, there are real functions $f_i\in L^\infty(\mu)$ with $\|f_i\|_\infty\le1$ and $\int f_i\,\,d\mu=0$, a finite set $F\subset[1,2]$, measurable functions $$N_a:C\longrightarrow\{c2^k:c\in F,\ k\ge a\},\qquad a\ge1,$$ a continuous real function $\Psi$ on $(\mathbb R/\mathbb Z)^I$, and $\epsilon_0>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $a$, $$\label{eq:selected-positive}
 \int_C\frac1{N_a(z)}\int_0^{N_a(z)}
 \Psi\bigl((\upsilon(S_C^{it}z))_{i\in I}\bigr)
 \prod_{i\in I}f_i\bigl(\xi(S_C^{it}z)\bigr)
 \,\,dt\,\,dm_C(z)\ge\epsilon_0.$$ The selectors may be chosen independent of the initial phase.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-selectors"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-selectors</summary>

*Proof.* Expand each function in the failed assertion into its mean and its centred part. Lemma <a href="#lem:lower-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lower-length">2</a> and the $L^2$ conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:relative-norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:relative-norm">4</a> show that every proper subaverage converges almost surely to the product of its means. Thus a fourfold average of centred functions fails to converge to zero. Expanding real and imaginary parts and scaling gives a real tuple bounded by one with the same property. Its averages $$A_N(x)=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\prod_{i\in I}f_i(T^{in}x)$$ converge to zero in $L^2$. After changing the sign of one function if necessary, there are $\delta>0$ and a measurable set $E$ of positive measure such that $A_N(x)>2\delta$ for arbitrarily large $N$, for every $x\in E$.

For bounded summands, changing the averaging length from $N$ to $M$ changes the average by at most $2|N-M|/\max(N,M)$. Choose a finite grid $F\subset[1,2]$ fine enough that arbitrarily late inequalities $A_N(x)>2\delta$ yield arbitrarily late lengths $L=c2^k$ satisfying $A_{\lfloor L\rfloor}(x)>\delta$. The rounding error tends to zero uniformly as $k\to\infty$. For $x\in E$, choose measurably, from a fixed enumeration, one such length with $k\ge a$. Set $N_a(x,u)$ equal to that choice. For $x\notin E$, set $N_a(x,u)=2^a$, including $1$ in $F$. Countability of the available lengths gives measurable selectors.

Choose nonnegative continuous functions $\alpha,\beta$ on $\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$, not identically zero, with supports respectively in $(1/4,1/2)$ and $(0,1/16)$. Define $$\Psi(v_1,v_2,v_3,v_4)
 =\alpha(2v_1-v_2)\,\beta(v_2-v_1).$$ For output phases $v_i=u+it\pmod1$, this equals $\alpha(u)\beta(\{t\})$. On its support, writing $t=n+s$ with $0\le s<1$ gives $0<u+is<1$ for every $i\in I$. Consequently the spatial outputs in <a href="#eq:selected-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-positive">[eq:selected-positive]</a> are precisely $T^{in}x$. If $b_\beta=\int_0^1\beta(s)\,\,ds$, integration over complete unit intervals yields, uniformly in $x,u$ and $L\ge2$, <a id="eq:phase-selector-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:phase-selector-comparison}
 \frac1L\int_0^L\Psi\bigl((u+it)_{i\in I}\bigr)
       \prod_i f_i\bigl(\xi(S_C^{it}(x,u))\bigr)\,\,dt
 =\alpha(u)b_\beta A_{\lfloor L\rfloor}(x)+O(L^{-1}).$$ The shift from the indices $0,\ldots,\lfloor L\rfloor-1$ to $1,\ldots,\lfloor L\rfloor$ costs another $O(L^{-1})$.

The integral of the right side over $E\times[0,1)$ is at least $\delta\mu(E)b_\beta\int\alpha+o(1)$. Over the complement of $E$ the selected length is deterministic, and $A_{2^a}\to0$ in $L^2$, so its contribution tends to zero. The constant in front of $\mu(E)$ is positive. Choosing $\epsilon_0$ smaller than its product with $\delta\mu(E)$ proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

For the rest of the proof assume failure and fix the functions, selectors and phase test of Proposition <a href="#prop:selectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:selectors">5</a>. We use one fixed free ultrafilter $\mathcal U$ on the indices $a$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A functorial law on four outputs

<a id="eq:selected-law"></a>
Consider a standard probability-preserving flow $(Y,m_Y,S_Y^t)$ with an equivariant factor $\pi_Y:Y\to C$. The selector on $Y$ is $N_a\circ\pi_Y$. For bounded measurable functions $G_i$ on $Y$, define the selected law by $$\label{eq:selected-law}
 \int_{Y^I}\prod_{i\in I}G_i(y_i)\,\,d\lambda_Y
 =\lim_{a\to\mathcal U}
 \int_Y\frac1{N_a(\pi_Yy)}
 \int_0^{N_a(\pi_Yy)}\prod_{i\in I}G_i(S_Y^{it}y)
 \,\,dt\,\,dm_Y(y).$$ The existence of a countably additive law with this formula is part of the next proposition.

Let $\omega_Y:Y\to W_Y$ be the maximal distal factor of time one, write $\nu_Y=(\omega_Y)_*m_Y$, and disintegrate $m_Y=\int m_{Y,w}\,\,d\nu_Y(w)$. For a state $(y_i)_{i\in I}$ of the selected law, put $P_i=\omega_Y(y_i)$ and $P=(P_i)_{i\in I}$.

<a id="stmt-prop-selected"></a>
<div id="prop:selected" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Selected laws). *Formula <a href="#eq:selected-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-law">[eq:selected-law]</a> defines a probability measure $\lambda_Y$ on $Y^I$ with the following properties.*

1.  *Every marginal is $m_Y$, and $\lambda_Y$ is invariant under $(y_i)_i\mapsto(S_Y^{it}y_i)_i$ for every $t\in\mathbb R$. If $\theta:Y'\to Y$ is an extension over $C$, then $(\theta^I)_*\lambda_{Y'}=\lambda_Y$. These projections also hold for countable inverse limits of extensions.*

<a id="eq:ordinary-parameter-law"></a>
2.  *The law $\Lambda_Y$ of $P$ is its deterministic-length law: for bounded $H_i$ on $W_Y$, $$\label{eq:ordinary-parameter-law}
     \int\prod_iH_i(w_i)\,\,d\Lambda_Y
     =\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_Y\frac1L\int_0^L
           \prod_i H_i(\omega_Y(S_Y^{it}y))\,\,dt\,\,dm_Y(y).$$*

<a id="eq:proper-fibres"></a>
3.  *For every proper subset $J\subsetneq I$, the conditional law of $(y_i)_{i\in J}$ given the *entire* parameter array $P$ is $$\label{eq:proper-fibres}
     \operatorname{Law}_{\lambda_Y}\bigl((y_i)_{i\in J}\mid P\bigr)
     =\bigotimes_{i\in J}m_{Y,P_i}.$$*

*In a countable inverse limit, functions pulled back from the selected laws at finite stages are dense in the $L^2$ space of the limiting selected law.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-selected"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-selected</summary>

*Proof.* Denote the finite-$a$ law in the right side of <a href="#eq:selected-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-law">[eq:selected-law]</a> by $\lambda_{Y,a}$. For a single bounded measurable function $G$, the ordinary pointwise ergodic theorem for the flow gives, since $N_a\ge2^a$, $$\frac1{N_a(\pi_Yy)}\int_0^{N_a(\pi_Yy)}G(S_Y^{it}y)\,\,dt
 \longrightarrow \mathbb E[G\mid\mathcal I_Y](y)$$ almost surely, where $\mathcal I_Y$ is the invariant factor of the flow. Integrating proves convergence of each marginal against every bounded measurable test to $m_Y$. Ergodicity is unnecessary.

Realize $Y$ as a Borel subset of a compact metrizable space $K$. The ultralimit of the integrals of continuous functions on $K^I$ is a positive functional of mass one, hence a probability measure. Its marginals are $m_Y$, so it is concentrated on $Y^I$. For bounded measurable slot functions, approximate each in $L^1(m_Y)$ by continuous functions on $K$, with the same uniform bound. The error in a product test is bounded by the sum of the errors in the individual marginal tests. The preceding marginal convergence makes this bound valid in the ultralimit as well as under the limiting measure. Letting the approximation errors tend to zero proves <a href="#eq:selected-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-law">[eq:selected-law]</a> for all bounded slot tests.

Applying the product action at time $s$ shifts the inner integration interval from $[0,N_a]$ to $[s,N_a+s]$. For tests bounded by one the error is at most $2|s|/2^a$. This proves invariance. Notice that the selector is still evaluated at the same initial state; no invariance of the selector is required. Pulling back product tests under an extension over $C$ leaves the selector unchanged and proves functoriality. A countable inverse limit is again a standard flow, and the same formula gives its stated projections. Its coordinate $\sigma$-fields are generated by the stage coordinate factors; finite products of their functions are dense, proving the last assertion. Strong continuity of the product flow in its new $L^2$ space follows first on tensors from the fixed marginal laws and then by density.

We next determine the parameter and proper-slot laws. The pointwise theorem for the distal system $W_Y$ passes to continuous time as follows. Write $t=n+s$, $0\le s<1$. For each fixed $s$ apply Proposition <a href="#prop:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:structure">3</a>(5) to the shifted functions $H_i\circ S_{W_Y}^{is}$. Fubini and bounded convergence give pointwise convergence after integrating $s$, while the final incomplete unit interval contributes $O(L^{-1})$. Thus the continuous averages converge almost surely along all real lengths. Their value at any selected length $N_a(\pi_Yy)$ has the same limit. Integration gives <a href="#eq:ordinary-parameter-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ordinary-parameter-law">[eq:ordinary-parameter-law]</a>.
